I’m an accountant, so everything is deadlines and review notes and little details, and now people are noticing I’m not keeping up. I mean, even my keyboard feels far away sometimes, which sounds stupid, but… yeah, that’s why I’m here.

job isn’t in danger, I can’t shake the certainty that I’ve already ruined us financially and that the firm is quietly building a case to expose me as incompetent.\n- key: S020\n  name: Hallucinations\n  description: Perception-like experiences that occur without an external stimulus and are as vivid and clear as normal perception.\n  manifestation: Sometimes when I open a spreadsheet or my email, I hear a clear voice like it’s coming from just behind my laptop saying, “You missed it, they know,” even though no one else is in the room.\n- key: S024\n  name: Depressed_Mood\n  description: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.\n  manifestation: Most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the day is already lost before I’ve even gotten out of bed.\n- key: S025\n  name: Loss_of_Interest\n  description: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.\n  manifestation: I used to plan hikes, take photos, cook on Sundays, and volunteer at the library, but now those things just feel like items on a checklist I don’t care enough to start.\n- key: S026\n  name: Weight_Appetite_Change\n  description: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.\n  manifestation: Food has mostly become something I force down because I know I should, and my work skirts have gotten loose even though I’m not trying to lose weight.\n- key: S029\n  name: Fatigue\n  description: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.\n  manifestation: Even after a full night in bed, my body feels like I’ve been up closing an audit for three days, and simple things like showering or sorting insurance paperwork can wipe me out.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur exclusively during the major depressive episode\n  manifestation: I never heard that voice or believed the firm was secretly building a case against me before this depression settled in; those experiences only started after my mood and energy had been low for a while.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: At work I’ve missed review deadlines and had to hand off parts of audits I normally manage myself, and at home my husband has taken over bills and insurance forms because I can’t reliably get through them.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 7 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about seven months, since my husband stopped working after the downsizing.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / accountant_or_auditor / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S028\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not really. I used to be the person who’d plan hikes weeks ahead, like I’d check the weather and pick a trail and make sure I had snacks—kind of ridiculous, honestly. I’d take photos too, mostly trees and rocks, nothing amazing. And Sundays I used to cook, like actually cook, not just heat something up.\n\nNow it all just feels like… tasks. Like volunteering at the library, even that, which I used to really like, just sits in my head like another item on a list I can’t make myself start. I don’t feel excited about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yes… all the time. It’s not just like, “Oh, I made a mistake,” it’s like I’m sure I’ve ruined everything. My husband will literally show me the bank balance and say we’re okay, and then I’ll remember some random charge from three months ago, like groceries or a parking ticket, and I’ll think, no, this is how it starts, this is how I’ve destroyed us.\n\nAnd at work… my manager says my job isn’t in danger, but I don’t believe it. I keep thinking the firm is quietly putting together proof that I’m incompetent, like they’re just waiting to expose me. I mean, even my keyboard feels far away sometimes, which sounds stupid, but… yeah, that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, “sad” sounds almost too small for it, but yes. Most days I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the day is already ruined before I’ve even gotten out of bed. And then I start thinking about emails, and my neighbor’s dog starts barking through the wall—sorry, that’s not really the point—but it’s like everything is already too much right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not really. I used to be the person who’d plan hikes weeks ahead, like I’d check the weather and pick a trail and make sure I had snacks—kind of ridiculous, honestly. I’d take photos too, mostly trees and rocks, nothing amazing. And Sundays I used to cook, like actually cook, not just heat something up.\n\nNow it all just feels like… tasks. Like volunteering at the library, even that, which I used to really like, just sits in my head like another item on a list I can’t make myself start. I don’t feel excited about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yes… all the time. It’s not just like, “Oh, I made a mistake,” it’s like I’m sure I’ve ruined everything. My husband will literally show me the bank balance and say we’re okay, and then I’ll remember some random charge from three months ago, like groceries or a parking ticket, and I’ll think, no, this is how it starts, this is how I’ve destroyed us.\n\nAnd at work… my manager says my job isn’t in danger, but I don’t believe it. I keep thinking the firm is quietly putting together proof that I’m incompetent, like they’re just waiting to expose me. I know people say that’s not happening, but I can’t shake it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I mean, I wouldn’t say “on edge” exactly, or maybe I would, I’m not sure what counts. Sometimes I feel like I’m bracing for something, but then I also think maybe everyone feels that way when they have too many emails and the apartment is a mess.\n\nIt’s hard to separate from everything else. Like yesterday I was standing in the kitchen and I couldn’t remember if I’d paid the electric bill, and then I started looking through old mail even though it turned out—well, I don’t even remember if that was the point. So… yes, maybe anxious, but I’m not sure how often or if that’s the right word.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve had moments where I think I’m so tired of everything being this hard, but then I get distracted by something stupid, like whether I left dishes in the sink or if I forgot to return a library book from months ago, and then I’m not even on the same thought anymore.\n\nHurting myself… I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve really— I mean, I haven’t sat down and made some kind of plan, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like everything feels heavy and I don’t know what I’m supposed to do with that. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, hollow feeling in your chest?\n\nPatient: I think… about seven months? It sort of started after my husband stopped working, when his company did that downsizing. I remember because I kept thinking we should’ve seen it coming, even though—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yes, around then. It’s been there most of the time since.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. That’s actually part of why I came in. I’ll be at work, looking at one audit workpaper, and I just… sit there. Like twenty minutes can go by before I even move my hand to type anything.\n\nAnd it’s embarrassing because, you know, numbers used to be the easy part for me—or at least structured, with columns and checkmarks and all that—and now I’m just staring at the screen like I’m waiting for it to do something. People at work have started noticing I’m not keeping up, which makes me want to explain, but I don’t even know what to say.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

My husband will literally show me the bank balance and say we’re okay, and then I’ll remember some random charge from three months ago, like groceries or a parking ticket, and I’ll think, no, this is how it starts, this is how I’ve destroyed us.\n\nAnd at work… my manager says my job isn’t in danger, but I don’t believe it. I keep thinking the firm is quietly putting together proof that I’m incompetent, like they’re just waiting to expose me. I know people say that’s not happening, but I can’t shake it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I mean, I wouldn’t say “on edge” exactly, or maybe I would, I’m not sure what counts. Sometimes I feel like I’m bracing for something, but then I also think maybe everyone feels that way when they have too many emails and the apartment is a mess.\n\nIt’s hard to separate from everything else. Like yesterday I was standing in the kitchen and I couldn’t remember if I’d paid the electric bill, and then I started looking through old mail even though it turned out—well, I don’t even remember if that was the point. So… yes, maybe anxious, but I’m not sure how often or if that’s the right word.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve had moments where I think I’m so tired of everything being this hard, but then I get distracted by something stupid, like whether I left dishes in the sink or if I forgot to return a library book from months ago, and then I’m not even on the same thought anymore.\n\nHurting myself… I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve really— I mean, I haven’t sat down and made some kind of plan, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like everything feels heavy and I don’t know what I’m supposed to do with that. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, hollow feeling in your chest?\n\nPatient: I think… about seven months? It sort of started after my husband stopped working, when his company did that downsizing. I remember because I kept thinking we should’ve seen it coming, even though—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yes, around then. It’s been there most of the time since.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. That’s actually part of why I came in. I’ll be at work, looking at one audit workpaper, and I just… sit there. Like twenty minutes can go by before I even move my hand to type anything.\n\nAnd it’s embarrassing because, you know, numbers used to be the easy part for me—or at least structured, with columns and checkmarks and all that—and now I’m just staring at the screen like I’m waiting for it to do something. People at work have started noticing I’m not keeping up, which makes me want to explain, but I don’t even know what to say.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I haven’t been weighing myself much, but my skirts for work—like the ones I always wear on audit days—have been sitting looser, and I keep having to adjust them. I thought maybe the waistband was just stretched out, but it’s more than one of them.\n\nFood just… it’s not really something I want. I make myself eat because I know I’m supposed to, or because it would be weird not to, but it feels like another task on a list. Like packing lunch used to be this whole routine, and now I’ll open the fridge and just stand there, and then somehow I’m looking at whether we’re out of paper towels instead. But yes, I think I’ve lost weight without trying.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about death frequently?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say frequently. I mean, sometimes I get this vague thought like… everything feels very far away, but then I start thinking about something else, like whether I paid the electric bill or if I left a spreadsheet open at work.\n\nSorry, I’m not answering that very clearly. I guess I’m not sure what counts as “thoughts about death.”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what brings me in is that I’ve gotten really slowed down. Like at work, I’ll be looking at one audit workpaper, just staring at it, and twenty minutes can go by before I even move my hand to type anything. It’s embarrassing. I’m an accountant, so everything is deadlines and review notes and little details, and now people are noticing I’m not keeping up. Most days I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the day is already ruined before I’ve even gotten out of bed. I used to be the person who’d plan hikes weeks ahead, like I’d check the weather and pick a trail and make sure I had snacks—kind of ridiculous, honestly. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what brings me in is that I’ve gotten really slowed down. Like at work, I’ll be looking at one audit workpaper, just staring at it, and twenty minutes can go by before I even move my hand to type anything. It’s embarrassing. I’m an accountant, so everything is deadlines and review notes and little details, and now people are noticing I’m not keeping up. I mean, even my keyboard feels far away sometimes, which sounds stupid, but… yeah, that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, “sad” sounds almost too small for it, but yes. Most days I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the day is already ruined before I’ve even gotten out of bed. And then I start thinking about emails, and my neighbor’s dog starts barking through the wall—sorry, that’s not really the point—but it’s like everything is already too much right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not really. I used to be the person who’d plan hikes weeks ahead, like I’d check the weather and pick a trail and make sure I had snacks—kind of ridiculous, honestly. I’d take photos too, mostly trees and rocks, nothing amazing. And Sundays I used to cook, like actually cook, not just heat something up.\n\nNow it all just feels like… tasks. Like volunteering at the library, even that, which I used to really like, just sits in my head like another item on a list I can’t make myself start. I don’t feel excited about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yes… all the time. It’s not just like, “Oh, I made a mistake,” it’s like I’m sure I’ve ruined everything. My husband will literally show me the bank balance and say we’re okay, and then I’ll remember some random charge from three months ago, like groceries or a parking ticket, and I’ll think, no, this is how it starts, this is how I’ve destroyed us.\n\nAnd at work… my manager says my job isn’t in danger, but I don’t believe it. I keep thinking the firm is quietly putting together proof that I’m incompetent, like they’re just waiting to expose me. I know people say that’s not happening, but I can’t shake it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I mean, I wouldn’t say “on edge” exactly, or maybe I would, I’m not sure what counts. Sometimes I feel like I’m bracing for something, but then I also think maybe everyone feels that way when they have too many emails and the apartment is a mess.\n\nIt’s hard to separate from everything else. Like yesterday I was standing in the kitchen and I couldn’t remember if I’d paid the electric bill, and then I started looking through old mail even though it turned out—well, I don’t even remember if that was the point. So… yes, maybe anxious, but I’m not sure how often or if that’s the right word.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve had moments where I think I’m so tired of everything being this hard, but then I get distracted by something stupid, like whether I left dishes in the sink or if I forgot to return a library book from months ago, and then I’m not even on the same thought anymore.\n\nHurting myself… I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve really— I mean, I haven’t sat down and made some kind of plan, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like everything feels heavy and I don’t know what I’m supposed to do with that. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, hollow feeling in your chest?\n\nPatient: I think… about seven months? It sort of started after my husband stopped working, when his company did that downsizing. I remember because I kept thinking we should’ve seen it coming, even though—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yes, around then. It’s been there most of the time since.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. That’s actually part of why I came in. I’ll be at work, looking at one audit workpaper, and I just… sit there. Like twenty minutes can go by before I even move my hand to type anything.\n\nAnd it’s embarrassing because, you know, numbers used to be the easy part for me—or at least structured, with columns and checkmarks and all that—and now I’m just staring at the screen like I’m waiting for it to do something. People at work have started noticing I’m not keeping up, which makes me want to explain, but I don’t even know what to say.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I haven’t been weighing myself much, but my skirts for work—like the ones I always wear on audit days—have been sitting looser, and I keep having to adjust them. I thought maybe the waistband was just stretched out, but it’s more than one of them.\n\nFood just… it’s not really something I want. I make myself eat because I know I’m supposed to, or because it would be weird not to, but it feels like another task on a list. Like packing lunch used to be this whole routine, and now I’ll open the fridge and just stand there, and then somehow I’m looking at whether we’re out of paper towels instead. But yes, I think I’ve lost weight without trying.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about death frequently?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say frequently. Not really, no. I mean, I used to have things I’d look forward to—like planning hikes on weekends, bringing my camera and taking pictures of stupid little things like moss or signs or whatever. And Sundays I used to cook, like actually pick a recipe and make a whole thing.

I also used to volunteer at the library sometimes, which sounds kind of boring when I say it out loud, but I liked it. The order of the shelves was… I don’t know, calming.

Now when I think about any of that, it just feels like another task. Like, “go hike,” “cook,” “send the email,” “fold laundry”—all in the same pile. I don’t really care enough to start.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.2, F33.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what brings me in is that I’ve gotten really slowed down. Like at work, I’ll be looking at one audit workpaper, just staring at it, and twenty minutes can go by before I even move my hand to type anything. It’s embarrassing. I’m an accountant, so everything is deadlines and review notes and little details, and now people are noticing I’m not keeping up. I mean, even my keyboard feels far away sometimes, which sounds stupid, but… yeah, that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, “sad” sounds almost too small for it, but yes. Most days I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the day is already ruined before I’ve even gotten out of bed. And then I start thinking about emails, and my neighbor’s dog starts barking through the wall—sorry, that’s not really the point—but it’s like everything is already too much right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not really. I used to be the person who’d plan hikes weeks ahead, like I’d check the weather and pick a trail and make sure I had snacks—kind of ridiculous, honestly. I’d take photos too, mostly trees and rocks, nothing amazing. And Sundays I used to cook, like actually cook, not just heat something up.\n\nNow it all just feels like… tasks. Like volunteering at the library, even that, which I used to really like, just sits in my head like another item on a list I can’t make myself start. I don’t feel excited about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yes… all the time. It’s not just like, “Oh, I made a mistake,” it’s like I’m sure I’ve ruined everything. My husband will literally show me the bank balance and say we’re okay, and then I’ll remember some random charge from three months ago, like groceries or a parking ticket, and I’ll think, no, this is how it starts, this is how I’ve destroyed us.\n\nAnd at work… my manager says my job isn’t in danger, but I don’t believe it. I keep thinking the firm is quietly putting together proof that I’m incompetent, like they’re just waiting to expose me. I know people say that’s not happening, but I can’t shake it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I mean, I wouldn’t say “on edge” exactly, or maybe I would, I’m not sure what counts. Sometimes I feel like I’m bracing for something, but then I also think maybe everyone feels that way when they have too many emails and the apartment is a mess.\n\nIt’s hard to separate from everything else. Like yesterday I was standing in the kitchen and I couldn’t remember if I’d paid the electric bill, and then I started looking through old mail even though it turned out—well, I don’t even remember if that was the point. So… yes, maybe anxious, but I’m not sure how often or if that’s the right word.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve had moments where I think I’m so tired of everything being this hard, but then I get distracted by something stupid, like whether I left dishes in the sink or if I forgot to return a library book from months ago, and then I’m not even on the same thought anymore.\n\nHurting myself… I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve really— I mean, I haven’t sat down and made some kind of plan, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like everything feels heavy and I don’t know what I’m supposed to do with that. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, hollow feeling in your chest?\n\nPatient: I think… about seven months? It sort of started after my husband stopped working, when his company did that downsizing. I remember because I kept thinking we should’ve seen it coming, even though—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yes, around then. It’s been there most of the time since.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. That’s actually part of why I came in. I’ll be at work, looking at one audit workpaper, and I just… sit there. Like twenty minutes can go by before I even move my hand to type anything.\n\nAnd it’s embarrassing because, you know, numbers used to be the easy part for me—or at least structured, with columns and checkmarks and all that—and now I’m just staring at the screen like I’m waiting for it to do something. People at work have started noticing I’m not keeping up, which makes me want to explain, but I don’t even know what to say.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I haven’t been weighing myself much, but my skirts for work—like the ones I always wear on audit days—have been sitting looser, and I keep having to adjust them. I thought maybe the waistband was just stretched out, but it’s more than one of them.\n\nFood just… it’s not really something I want. I make myself eat because I know I’m supposed to, or because it would be weird not to, but it feels like another task on a list. Like packing lunch used to be this whole routine, and now I’ll open the fridge and just stand there, and then somehow I’m looking at whether we’re out of paper towels instead. But yes, I think I’ve lost weight without trying.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about death frequently?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say frequently. I mean, sometimes I get this vague thought like… everything feels very far away, but then I start thinking about something else, like whether I paid the electric bill or if I left a spreadsheet open at work.\n\nSorry, I’m not answering that very clearly. I guess I’m not sure what counts as “thoughts about death.”\n\nDoctor: Have you been thinking about death a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. People at work have started noticing I’m not keeping up, which makes me want to explain, but I don’t even know what to say.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I haven’t been weighing myself much, but my skirts for work—like the ones I always wear on audit days—have been sitting looser, and I keep having to adjust them. I thought maybe the waistband was just stretched out, but it’s more than one of them.\n\nFood just… it’s not really something I want. I make myself eat because I know I’m supposed to, or because it would be weird not to, but it feels like another task on a list. Like packing lunch used to be this whole routine, and now I’ll open the fridge and just stand there, and then somehow I’m looking at whether we’re out of paper towels instead. But yes, I think I’ve lost weight without trying.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about death frequently?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say frequently. I mean, sometimes I get this vague thought like… everything feels very far away, but then I start thinking about something else, like whether I paid the electric bill or if I left a spreadsheet open at work.\n\nSorry, I’m not answering that very clearly. I guess I’m not sure what counts as “thoughts about death.”\n\nDoctor: Have you been thinking about death a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I don’t think I’ve been sitting there thinking about death all day, exactly. It’s more like the question makes me try to count it, and then I start wondering what counts.\n\nLike, if I’m lying in bed and thinking I don’t really want to get up, is that the same thing? Or if I’m watching some commercial and suddenly remembering I never called the insurance company back—that’s not related, sorry. I just… I don’t know how to measure “a lot.” I know I’ve been thinking about things in a heavier way, but death specifically… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that you would be better off dead?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know. “Better off dead” sounds so specific, and I don’t think I’ve been saying that to myself like a sentence.\n\nSometimes I think I’m just… not doing things right, or I’m making everything harder somehow, and then I’ll start worrying about whether I messed up some little thing at work, like a number not tying out, even if I checked it already. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I’m not sure. I don’t know if I’d call it that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had two weeks or more where you felt depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I haven’t been keeping track on a calendar or anything, but most days I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the day is already kind of ruined before I even get out of bed.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, okay, I need to shower, I need to check my email, I need to—sorry, that’s not what you asked. But yes, it’s been going on long enough that it doesn’t feel like just a bad day or two.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I used to have things I’d look forward to—like planning hikes on weekends, bringing my camera and taking pictures of stupid little things like moss or signs or whatever. And Sundays I used to cook, like actually pick a recipe and make a whole thing.\n\nI also used to volunteer at the library sometimes, which sounds kind of boring when I say it out loud, but I liked it. The order of the shelves was… I don’t know, calming.\n\nNow when I think about any of that, it just feels like another task. Like, “go hike,” “cook,” “send the email,” “fold laundry”—all in the same pile. I don’t really care enough to start.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I know people say that’s not happening, but I can’t shake it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I mean, I wouldn’t say “on edge” exactly, or maybe I would, I’m not sure what counts. Sometimes I feel like I’m bracing for something, but then I also think maybe everyone feels that way when they have too many emails and the apartment is a mess.\n\nIt’s hard to separate from everything else. Like yesterday I was standing in the kitchen and I couldn’t remember if I’d paid the electric bill, and then I started looking through old mail even though it turned out—well, I don’t even remember if that was the point. So… yes, maybe anxious, but I’m not sure how often or if that’s the right word.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve had moments where I think I’m so tired of everything being this hard, but then I get distracted by something stupid, like whether I left dishes in the sink or if I forgot to return a library book from months ago, and then I’m not even on the same thought anymore.\n\nHurting myself… I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve really— I mean, I haven’t sat down and made some kind of plan, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like everything feels heavy and I don’t know what I’m supposed to do with that. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, hollow feeling in your chest?\n\nPatient: I think… about seven months? It sort of started after my husband stopped working, when his company did that downsizing. I remember because I kept thinking we should’ve seen it coming, even though—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yes, around then. It’s been there most of the time since.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. That’s actually part of why I came in. I’ll be at work, looking at one audit workpaper, and I just… sit there. Like twenty minutes can go by before I even move my hand to type anything.\n\nAnd it’s embarrassing because, you know, numbers used to be the easy part for me—or at least structured, with columns and checkmarks and all that—and now I’m just staring at the screen like I’m waiting for it to do something. People at work have started noticing I’m not keeping up, which makes me want to explain, but I don’t even know what to say.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I haven’t been weighing myself much, but my skirts for work—like the ones I always wear on audit days—have been sitting looser, and I keep having to adjust them. I thought maybe the waistband was just stretched out, but it’s more than one of them.\n\nFood just… it’s not really something I want. I make myself eat because I know I’m supposed to, or because it would be weird not to, but it feels like another task on a list. Like packing lunch used to be this whole routine, and now I’ll open the fridge and just stand there, and then somehow I’m looking at whether we’re out of paper towels instead. But yes, I think I’ve lost weight without trying.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about death frequently?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say frequently. I mean, sometimes I get this vague thought like… everything feels very far away, but then I start thinking about something else, like whether I paid the electric bill or if I left a spreadsheet open at work.\n\nSorry, I’m not answering that very clearly. I guess I’m not sure what counts as “thoughts about death.”\n\nDoctor: Have you been thinking about death a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I don’t think I’ve been sitting there thinking about death all day, exactly. It’s more like the question makes me try to count it, and then I start wondering what counts.\n\nLike, if I’m lying in bed and thinking I don’t really want to get up, is that the same thing? Or if I’m watching some commercial and suddenly remembering I never called the insurance company back—that’s not related, sorry. I just… I don’t know how to measure “a lot.” I know I’ve been thinking about things in a heavier way, but death specifically… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that you would be better off dead?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know. “Better off dead” sounds so specific, and I don’t think I’ve been saying that to myself like a sentence.\n\nSometimes I think I’m just… not doing things right, or I’m making everything harder somehow, and then I’ll start worrying about whether I messed up some little thing at work, like a number not tying out, even if I checked it already. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I’m not sure. I don’t know if I’d call it that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had two weeks or more where you felt depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I haven’t been keeping track on a calendar or anything, but most days I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the day is already kind of ruined before I even get out of bed.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, okay, I need to shower, I need to check my email, I need to—sorry, that’s not what you asked. But yes, it’s been going on long enough that it doesn’t feel like just a bad day or two.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I used to have things I’d look forward to—like planning hikes on weekends, bringing my camera and taking pictures of stupid little things like moss or signs or whatever. And Sundays I used to cook, like actually pick a recipe and make a whole thing.\n\nI also used to volunteer at the library sometimes, which sounds kind of boring when I say it out loud, but I liked it. The order of the shelves was… I don’t know, calming.\n\nNow when I think about any of that, it just feels like another task. Like, “go hike,” “cook,” “send the email,” “fold laundry”—all in the same pile. I don’t really care enough to start.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of depression like this before?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’ve had bad stretches before, like everyone does, I guess… finals in college were awful, and tax season can make time feel weird, like you’re living under fluorescent lights forever.\n\nBut I don’t know if it was like this. I wasn’t really measuring it, and I tend to just sort of push through and then later think, “Was that normal?” So… I don’t know. Maybe, but I can’t say for sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Like twenty minutes can go by before I even move my hand to type anything.\n\nAnd it’s embarrassing because, you know, numbers used to be the easy part for me—or at least structured, with columns and checkmarks and all that—and now I’m just staring at the screen like I’m waiting for it to do something. People at work have started noticing I’m not keeping up, which makes me want to explain, but I don’t even know what to say.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I haven’t been weighing myself much, but my skirts for work—like the ones I always wear on audit days—have been sitting looser, and I keep having to adjust them. I thought maybe the waistband was just stretched out, but it’s more than one of them.\n\nFood just… it’s not really something I want. I make myself eat because I know I’m supposed to, or because it would be weird not to, but it feels like another task on a list. Like packing lunch used to be this whole routine, and now I’ll open the fridge and just stand there, and then somehow I’m looking at whether we’re out of paper towels instead. But yes, I think I’ve lost weight without trying.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about death frequently?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say frequently. I mean, sometimes I get this vague thought like… everything feels very far away, but then I start thinking about something else, like whether I paid the electric bill or if I left a spreadsheet open at work.\n\nSorry, I’m not answering that very clearly. I guess I’m not sure what counts as “thoughts about death.”\n\nDoctor: Have you been thinking about death a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I don’t think I’ve been sitting there thinking about death all day, exactly. It’s more like the question makes me try to count it, and then I start wondering what counts.\n\nLike, if I’m lying in bed and thinking I don’t really want to get up, is that the same thing? Or if I’m watching some commercial and suddenly remembering I never called the insurance company back—that’s not related, sorry. I just… I don’t know how to measure “a lot.” I know I’ve been thinking about things in a heavier way, but death specifically… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that you would be better off dead?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know. “Better off dead” sounds so specific, and I don’t think I’ve been saying that to myself like a sentence.\n\nSometimes I think I’m just… not doing things right, or I’m making everything harder somehow, and then I’ll start worrying about whether I messed up some little thing at work, like a number not tying out, even if I checked it already. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I’m not sure. I don’t know if I’d call it that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had two weeks or more where you felt depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I haven’t been keeping track on a calendar or anything, but most days I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the day is already kind of ruined before I even get out of bed.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, okay, I need to shower, I need to check my email, I need to—sorry, that’s not what you asked. And Sundays I used to cook, like actually pick a recipe and make a whole thing.\n\nI also used to volunteer at the library sometimes, which sounds kind of boring when I say it out loud, but I liked it. The order of the shelves was… I don’t know, calming.\n\nNow when I think about any of that, it just feels like another task. Like, “go hike,” “cook,” “send the email,” “fold laundry”—all in the same pile. I don’t really care enough to start.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of depression like this before?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’ve had bad stretches before, like everyone does, I guess… finals in college were awful, and tax season can make time feel weird, like you’re living under fluorescent lights forever.\n\nBut I don’t know if it was like this. I wasn’t really measuring it, and I tend to just sort of push through and then later think, “Was that normal?” So… I don’t know. Maybe, but I can’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I keep trying to sort my life into neat little sections, like “that was a depressed period” versus “that was just a hard month,” and it’s not that clean.\n\nThere were times before when I felt low, I guess, but whether it was like this… I can’t really say. It all kind of blurs together when I look back. Like I remember being miserable during certain stretches, but then I also remember still functioning, going to work, making spreadsheets, buying groceries—actually, I used to make these very elaborate grocery lists, sorted by aisle, which is probably ridiculous—\n\nSorry. I’m not answering clearly. I’m not sure if there’s been more than one period like this. Maybe, but I don’t trust my memory enough to say yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed like this before the current episode?\n\nPatient: I think… I don’t know that I can say that clearly. I’m sorry, I know you’re asking for yes or no, but when I look back it gets kind of messy.\n\nThere were times I was down before, definitely, but I don’t know if they were “like this.” I tend to keep going and then only later realize I was not doing well. It’s like when you’re in a room with bad lighting and you don’t notice until you step outside—sorry, that’s a weird comparison.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I can’t confidently say there was more than one period like this before now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure. I mean, I think there have been times where I seemed okay, or at least I was doing the normal things—work, errands, answering emails, making dinner if you count microwaving something as dinner.

But “normal” is hard for me to judge. Sometimes I only realize I wasn’t doing well after the fact, and then I start second-guessing the better times too. Like, was I actually better, or just busy? I don’t know. It’s kind of like tax season ending and suddenly everyone acts like life goes back to normal, but your brain is still… catching up.

So I guess I can’t say clearly. Maybe there were better stretches, but I don’t know if they were really normal.

manifestation: Even after a full night in bed, my body feels like I’ve been up closing an audit for three days, and simple things like showering or sorting insurance paperwork can wipe me out.\n- key: I’m not sure. Two months sounds so specific, and I don’t really track it that way. I can remember stretches where I was getting through things and maybe felt less weighed down, but whether that was a full two months of not feeling depressed… I honestly don’t know.

Most days I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the day is already ruined before I’ve even gotten out of bed. I used to be the person who’d plan hikes weeks ahead, like I’d check the weather and pick a trail and make sure I had snacks—kind of ridiculous, honestly. I’d take photos too, mostly trees and rocks, nothing amazing. And Sundays I used to cook, like actually cook, not just heat something up.\n\nNow it all just feels like… tasks. I don’t feel excited about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yes… all the time. It’s not just like, “Oh, I made a mistake,” it’s like I’m sure I’ve ruined everything. My husband will literally show me the bank balance and say we’re okay, and then I’ll remember some random charge from three months ago, like groceries or a parking ticket, and I’ll think, no, this is how it starts, this is how I’ve destroyed us.\n\nAnd at work… my manager says my job isn’t in danger, but I don’t believe it. I keep thinking the firm is quietly putting together proof that I’m incompetent, like they’re just waiting to expose me. I know people say that’s not happening, but I can’t shake it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I mean, I wouldn’t say “on edge” exactly, or maybe I would, I’m not sure what counts. Sometimes I feel like I’m bracing for something, but then I also think maybe everyone feels that way when they have too many emails and the apartment is a mess.\n\nIt’s hard to separate from everything else. Like yesterday I was standing in the kitchen and I couldn’t remember if I’d paid the electric bill, and then I started looking through old mail even though it turned out—well, I don’t even remember if that was the point. So… yes, maybe anxious, but I’m not sure how often or if that’s the right word.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that. I don’t think I’ve really— I mean, I haven’t sat down and made some kind of plan, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like everything feels heavy and I don’t know what I’m supposed to do with that. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, hollow feeling in your chest?\n\nPatient: I think… about seven months? People at work have started noticing I’m not keeping up, which makes me want to explain, but I don’t even know what to say.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I haven’t been weighing myself much, but my skirts for work—like the ones I always wear on audit days—have been sitting looser, and I keep having to adjust them. I thought maybe the waistband was just stretched out, but it’s more than one of them.\n\nFood just… it’s not really something I want. I make myself eat because I know I’m supposed to, or because it would be weird not to, but it feels like another task on a list. Like packing lunch used to be this whole routine, and now I’ll open the fridge and just stand there, and then somehow I’m looking at whether we’re out of paper towels instead. But yes, I think I’ve lost weight without trying.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about death frequently?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say frequently. I mean, sometimes I get this vague thought like… everything feels very far away, but then I start thinking about something else, like whether I paid the electric bill or if I left a spreadsheet open at work.\n\nSorry, I’m not answering that very clearly. I guess I’m not sure what counts as “thoughts about death.”\n\nDoctor: Have you been thinking about death a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I don’t think I’ve been sitting there thinking about death all day, exactly. It’s more like the question makes me try to count it, and then I start wondering what counts.\n\nLike, if I’m lying in bed and thinking I don’t really want to get up, is that the same thing? Or if I’m watching some commercial and suddenly remembering I never called the insurance company back—that’s not related, sorry. I just… I don’t know how to measure “a lot.” I know I’ve been thinking about things in a heavier way, but death specifically… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that you would be better off dead?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know. “Better off dead” sounds so specific, and I don’t think I’ve been saying that to myself like a sentence.\n\nSometimes I think I’m just… not doing things right, or I’m making everything harder somehow, and then I’ll start worrying about whether I messed up some little thing at work, like a number not tying out, even if I checked it already. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I’m not sure. I don’t know if I’d call it that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had two weeks or more where you felt depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I haven’t been keeping track on a calendar or anything, but most days I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the day is already kind of ruined before I even get out of bed.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, okay, I need to shower, I need to check my email, I need to—sorry, that’s not what you asked. But yes, it’s been going on long enough that it doesn’t feel like just a bad day or two.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I used to have things I’d look forward to—like planning hikes on weekends, bringing my camera and taking pictures of stupid little things like moss or signs or whatever. And Sundays I used to cook, like actually pick a recipe and make a whole thing.\n\nI also used to volunteer at the library sometimes, which sounds kind of boring when I say it out loud, but I liked it. The order of the shelves was… I don’t know, calming.\n\nNow when I think about any of that, it just feels like another task. Like, “go hike,” “cook,” “send the email,” “fold laundry”—all in the same pile. I don’t really care enough to start.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of depression like this before?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’ve had bad stretches before, like everyone does, I guess… finals in college were awful, and tax season can make time feel weird, like you’re living under fluorescent lights forever.\n\nBut I don’t know if it was like this. I wasn’t really measuring it, and I tend to just sort of push through and then later think, “Was that normal?” So… I don’t know. Maybe, but I can’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. People at work have started noticing I’m not keeping up, which makes me want to explain, but I don’t even know what to say.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I haven’t been weighing myself much, but my skirts for work—like the ones I always wear on audit days—have been sitting looser, and I keep having to adjust them. I thought maybe the waistband was just stretched out, but it’s more than one of them.\n\nFood just… it’s not really something I want. I make myself eat because I know I’m supposed to, or because it would be weird not to, but it feels like another task on a list. Like packing lunch used to be this whole routine, and now I’ll open the fridge and just stand there, and then somehow I’m looking at whether we’re out of paper towels instead. But yes, I think I’ve lost weight without trying.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about death frequently?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say frequently. I mean, sometimes I get this vague thought like… everything feels very far away, but then I start thinking about something else, like whether I paid the electric bill or if I left a spreadsheet open at work.\n\nSorry, I’m not answering that very clearly. I guess I’m not sure what counts as “thoughts about death.”\n\nDoctor: Have you been thinking about death a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I don’t think I’ve been sitting there thinking about death all day, exactly. It’s more like the question makes me try to count it, and then I start wondering what counts.\n\nLike, if I’m lying in bed and thinking I don’t really want to get up, is that the same thing? Or if I’m watching some commercial and suddenly remembering I never called the insurance company back—that’s not related, sorry. I just… I don’t know how to measure “a lot.” I know I’ve been thinking about things in a heavier way, but death specifically… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that you would be better off dead?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know. “Better off dead” sounds so specific, and I don’t think I’ve been saying that to myself like a sentence.\n\nSometimes I think I’m just… not doing things right, or I’m making everything harder somehow, and then I’ll start worrying about whether I messed up some little thing at work, like a number not tying out, even if I checked it already. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I’m not sure. I don’t know if I’d call it that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had two weeks or more where you felt depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I haven’t been keeping track on a calendar or anything, but most days I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the day is already kind of ruined before I even get out of bed.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, okay, I need to shower, I need to check my email, I need to—sorry, that’s not what you asked. But yes, it’s been going on long enough that it doesn’t feel like just a bad day or two.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I used to have things I’d look forward to—like planning hikes on weekends, bringing my camera and taking pictures of stupid little things like moss or signs or whatever. And Sundays I used to cook, like actually pick a recipe and make a whole thing.\n\nI also used to volunteer at the library sometimes, which sounds kind of boring when I say it out loud, but I liked it. The order of the shelves was… I don’t know, calming.\n\nNow when I think about any of that, it just feels like another task. Like, “go hike,” “cook,” “send the email,” “fold laundry”—all in the same pile. I don’t really care enough to start.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of depression like this before?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’ve had bad stretches before, like everyone does, I guess… finals in college were awful, and tax season can make time feel weird, like you’re living under fluorescent lights forever.\n\nBut I don’t know if it was like this. I wasn’t really measuring it, and I tend to just sort of push through and then later think, “Was that normal?” So… I don’t know. Maybe, but I can’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I keep trying to sort my life into neat little sections, like “that was a depressed period” versus “that was just a hard month,” and it’s not that clean.\n\nThere were times before when I felt low, I guess, but whether it was like this… I can’t really say. It all kind of blurs together when I look back. Like I remember being miserable during certain stretches, but then I also remember still functioning, going to work, making spreadsheets, buying groceries—actually, I used to make these very elaborate grocery lists, sorted by aisle, which is probably ridiculous—\n\nSorry. I’m not answering clearly. I’m not sure if there’s been more than one period like this. Maybe, but I don’t trust my memory enough to say yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed like this before the current episode?\n\nPatient: I think… I don’t know that I can say that clearly. I’m sorry, I know you’re asking for yes or no, but when I look back it gets kind of messy.\n\nThere were times I was down before, definitely, but I don’t know if they were “like this.” I tend to keep going and then only later realize I was not doing well. It’s like when you’re in a room with bad lighting and you don’t notice until you step outside—sorry, that’s a weird comparison.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I can’t confidently say there was more than one period like this before now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of feeling normal, or at least much better, between episodes of depression?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I think there have been times where I seemed okay, or at least I was doing the normal things—work, errands, answering emails, making dinner if you count microwaving something as dinner.\n\nBut “normal” is hard for me to judge. Sometimes I only realize I wasn’t doing well after the fact, and then I start second-guessing the better times too. Like, was I actually better, or just busy? I don’t know. It’s kind of like tax season ending and suddenly everyone acts like life goes back to normal, but your brain is still… catching up.\n\nSo I guess I can’t say clearly. Maybe there were better stretches, but I don’t know if they were really normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two months where you did not feel depressed before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Two months sounds so specific, and I don’t really track it that way. I don’t really care enough to start.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of depression like this before?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’ve had bad stretches before, like everyone does, I guess… finals in college were awful, and tax season can make time feel weird, like you’re living under fluorescent lights forever.\n\nBut I don’t know if it was like this. I wasn’t really measuring it, and I tend to just sort of push through and then later think, “Was that normal?” So… I don’t know. Maybe, but I can’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I keep trying to sort my life into neat little sections, like “that was a depressed period” versus “that was just a hard month,” and it’s not that clean.\n\nThere were times before when I felt low, I guess, but whether it was like this… I can’t really say. It all kind of blurs together when I look back. Like I remember being miserable during certain stretches, but then I also remember still functioning, going to work, making spreadsheets, buying groceries—actually, I used to make these very elaborate grocery lists, sorted by aisle, which is probably ridiculous—\n\nSorry. I’m not answering clearly. I’m not sure if there’s been more than one period like this. Maybe, but I don’t trust my memory enough to say yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed like this before the current episode?\n\nPatient: I think… I don’t know that I can say that clearly. I’m sorry, I know you’re asking for yes or no, but when I look back it gets kind of messy.\n\nThere were times I was down before, definitely, but I don’t know if they were “like this.” I tend to keep going and then only later realize I was not doing well. It’s like when you’re in a room with bad lighting and you don’t notice until you step outside—sorry, that’s a weird comparison.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I can’t confidently say there was more than one period like this before now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of feeling normal, or at least much better, between episodes of depression?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I think there have been times where I seemed okay, or at least I was doing the normal things—work, errands, answering emails, making dinner if you count microwaving something as dinner.\n\nBut “normal” is hard for me to judge. Sometimes I only realize I wasn’t doing well after the fact, and then I start second-guessing the better times too. Like, was I actually better, or just busy? I don’t know. It’s kind of like tax season ending and suddenly everyone acts like life goes back to normal, but your brain is still… catching up.\n\nSo I guess I can’t say clearly. Maybe there were better stretches, but I don’t know if they were really normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two months where you did not feel depressed before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Two months sounds so specific, and I don’t really track it that way. I can remember stretches where I was getting through things and maybe felt less weighed down, but whether that was a full two months of not feeling depressed… I honestly don’t know.\n\nI mean, my work calendar would probably know more than I do, because everything is in there—deadlines, meetings, reminders to renew my parking pass, all these tiny things—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… I can’t say for sure that there was a two-month period like that before this started.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before the current one that started about seven months ago?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know.
